Course: "21st Century English for Libya — Primary 5
Pupil's Book" on its cover — but one honest note, as
printed: inside the book the children say **"We're in
Grade 4 now!"** and the youngest says "I'm in Grade 3."
After the grade-04 re-cover found above, this book's
cover grade and its inside grade differ by one; its
content is new (not Grade 3's), and it is filed here
under its library grade, Grade 5, with the inside words
recorded as printed. The year's new names: **Yousif**
(9) and his sister **Lina** (8); Kareem and Nadia —
Grade 2's children — are back at 9, and the language
takes its biggest step yet: comparatives (taller than,
older than), possessive questions (Whose rubber is
this? — It's Yousif's.), countries and capitals,
months, money (Libyan Dinars, 2.5), going-to future,
There was / There were, Have you got…?, and must /
mustn't — all in the Book Map's own language list.

### Grade 5 Pupils Book — unit by unit

**Unit 1 "Our School" (book pp.7–24)**. L1 **New
Friends** (pp.8–9): four children introduce themselves
as reading texts, and the comparisons are inside them:
Yousif — "Hello, I'm Yousif and I'm 9. I'm tall for my
age. I live with my mum, dad and my sister, Lina. She's
8." · Kareem — "Hi, I'm Kareem. I'm 9. Yousif is my best
friend. I have very short black hair. My favourite
sport is running. Yousif is taller than me, but I am
bigger." · Nadia — "My name is Nadia. I am 9, so I am
older than Lina. She's my best friend. I have long
black hair." · Lina — "Hello, I'm Lina. I'm in Grade 3
at school and I'm 8. I have long hair. My favourite
lesson is English and my favourite colour is red." The
classroom texts add "Here's our new classroom. We're
in Grade 4 now!" and "I'm younger – I'm only 8, so I'm
in Grade 3. My classroom is next door.", then questions
asked and checked by listening ("How old is Nadia? —
Nadia is 9."). L2 **Our Playground** (pp.10–11): the
-ing spellings shown as pairs (walk – walking, play –
playing, **spin – spinning**), the playground set
(tunnel, bars, swing, roundabout, slide, ladder), and
sentences to finish: "Look at me! I'm flying up on the
swings. · I'm walking along the bars. · I'm climbing
up the ladder. · I'm on my knees. I'm crawling through
the tunnel. · I'm going down the slide. · I'm spinning
around on the roundabout." — with the movement words
up, through, around, down, along in a box. L3
**Playground Rules** (pp.12–13): the rules in print —
"Do not stand on the swing. · Do not go too fast. ·
Hold on with both hands. · Do not push. · Do not
balance on 1 leg." — a Let's check! box that teaches
**do not = don't**, and **The playground song** in
full: "Playing in the playground, / lots of games to
play. / Playing in the playground. / We do it every
day. / Climbing up the ladder. / Going down the slide.
/ Crawling through the tunnel, / a clever place to
hide!" (twice over). L4 **What's in my school bag?**
(pp.14–15): there is / there are with **some**, as a
guessing game over the two bags ("there is a rubber
and a pencil · there are some books · there is some
paper · there are some crayons…"), an Are-they-the-
same? check ("I'm 8. / I'm 40." · "I'm 138 cm. / I'm
146 cm."), and choose-two-words describing (long, big,
tall, young, short, old, small). L5 **What's the
difference?** (pp.16–17): comparisons taught head-on —
"This ruler is longer." · "I am younger than Yousif."
— the Opposites table spoken aloud (long–longer, tall–
taller, short–shorter, old–older, big–bigger, young–
younger, small–smaller), the rhyme **Bigger and
smaller** in full ("A ruler is longer than a pen, / And
number 12 is bigger than 10. / A giraffe is taller than
a bee, / And my mum and dad are older than me. / So I
am younger than them, you see! / A car is smaller than
a van, / And I am shorter than that man. / Let's say
these words together again: / Longer, shorter, / Older,
younger, / Bigger, smaller, / Taller, shorter."), and
make-your-own sentences ("I am taller than ___. · My
mum is ___ than me."). L6 **Packing My School Bag**
(pp.18–19): two emails read — Kareem to Yousif: "Hello
Yousif / Can you help me, please? I can't find my
timetable. What lessons do we have tomorrow? It's
Tuesday tomorrow and I think we have PE. / Kareem" —
and the reply: "Hi Kareem / First it's science. Then,
it's Arabic. After that, it's RE. See you at school. /
Yousif" (First / Then / After that now order a school
day), and **Whose …?** — "Whose rubber is this? — It's
Lina's." · "Whose rulers are these? — They're Lina's."
L7 **At the School Shop** (pp.20–21): shop words
(sharpeners, sweets, lemonade, apples, pencils, milk,
cookies, notebooks) and the shelves — top, middle,
bottom: "Let's put the apple on the middle shelf. ·
No, it's better on the bottom shelf." / "Let's put the
pencils on the bottom shelf. — Yes, that's good. / No,
they're better on the table." L8 Story: **The Lion
and the Mouse** (pp.22–23), printed in full with the
unit's language inside it: "Once, there was a big
lion. One day, a small mouse ran over the lion's head.
The lion caught the mouse. 'You are smaller than me
and **I'm going to eat you**,' he said. 'Let me go,'
said the mouse. 'I am smaller than you, but I may help
you one day.' The lion laughed. 'You are **too small
to help me** and you are too small to eat!' he said.
Because the lion was feeling happy, he let the mouse
go. Later that day, the lion went for a walk. He saw
the net, but it was too late and he was caught. The
lion roared and roared. The mouse heard the lion. '**I
must help my friend**,' said the mouse. 'How can you
help me?' said the lion. He was very sad. '**I will
show you**,' said the mouse. The mouse started chewing
the net. At last the lion was free! 'Thank you,' said
the lion. 'You are smaller than me, but you are a good
friend.'" "Now I can" (p.24): introduce and talk about
myself · name playground things · say what people are
doing in the playground · talk about playground rules
· talk about my school bag · talk about differences ·
ask and answer with whose · say where things are on
shelves.

**Unit 2 "Around the World" (book pp.25–42)**. L1
**Where are you from?** (pp.26–27): children of the
world on a labelled map (North America, South
America, Europe, Asia, Africa, Middle East, Australia)
— Sam from England, Liz from America, Pedro and Alex
from Brazil, Anna from Russia, Amy from China, Adel
from Libya: "Hello. My name's Sam. I'm from England.
Where are you from?" A Let's check! box teaches
**Names have capital letters** (Yousif, Lina, Libya,
China, England). L2 **I'm from Libya** (pp.28–29) is a
great map of Libya and its neighbours; Adel says "I'm
from Libya. I live in Sabha. What about you?", and
another voice "I'm from England. I live in London."
L3 **Are they the same?** (pp.30–31): an **Information
file** comparing England and Libya — the size of the
country; the weather in numbers (summer: Libya 30°C,
England 19°C; winter: Libya 19°C, England 7°C) — with
statements to judge true or false ("England is
smaller than Libya. · Summer in Libya is hotter. ·
Winter in England is hotter.") and the **hundreds
rhyme**: "One hundred, two hundred / Three hundred,
four … / Five hundred, six hundred / Seven hundred,
more … / Eight hundred, nine hundred / Ten hundred?
NO! / We say one thousand, one thousand 1,000 / Now
let's all have a go!" + "What's the number? — It's
300." L4 **Different Days** (pp.32–33): two cloze texts
with word banks — **My school day by Adel** ("Today is
Tuesday. I go to school at 8 o'clock. I go to school
on foot. There are 900 children in my school. On
Tuesday, I have Arabic, PE, maths and **history**. I
have my lunch at home. I play with my baby sister and
brother.") and **My weekend by Liz** (Saturday shopping
with mum at 10 o'clock, by bus, lots of cars and
shops; Sunday in the park — swing, slide, bars,
roundabout; lunch in a café; home at 3 o'clock; "I
like rice and chicken.") — each ending "What about
you?" with the child's own lines to write. L5 **About
Time** (pp.34–35): a homework dialog that teaches the
half hour — "It's 3 o'clock. It's 3.30. — Can you help
me? — Yes, I'll try." … "Look. Count the minutes. 5,
10, 15, 20, 25, 30, 35, 40, 45, 50, 55, 60. I see. 3
and 30 minutes. It's 3.30!" — minutes counted in fives.
L6 **Geography: Country Profile** (pp.36–37): the land
set (mountains, stream, **oil field**, desert, village,
river, lake, field, city), the compass points North,
South, East, West, and a profile of Libya read and
numbered: "Libya is a big country. It has high
mountains and dry desert. Libya has the 3rd largest
desert in the world. The Sahara desert is as big as
the United States. Libya has big cities and small
villages. It has green fields for farming. There is
lots of oil in Libya." — adjective matching under it
(long, big, high, green, dry, small). L7 **Guess what
it is!** (pp.38–39): the clue game grown to land
words — a tree ("It is green and brown. It is tall. It
has leaves."), a village ("It is small and it is in
the country. People live here in houses and on
farms."), a mountain ("It is very big. People climb
it. It can have snow at the top."), oil ("It comes
from under the ground. It is black. We need it for
cars and trucks."). L8 Story: **City Mouse and Village
Mouse** (pp.40–41), told in print: the friends visit
each way; the picnic of bread and cheese — "This food
is boring. City food is better." — the city feast
("Village Mouse ate and ate. There was lots of food
and it was very rich."), then "The door opened. Meow!
A big, scary, orange cat jumped up to the table.
'Help! Run!' City Mouse and Village Mouse ran as fast
as they could. When they were safe, Village Mouse
said, 'I don't like the city. I am going home to the
country. It's quieter in the village.'" and home to "a
lovely lunch of bread and cheese. I love my simple
food." "Now I can" (p.42): talk about countries · say
where I am from and talk about my country · compare
cities · say numbers to 1,000 · talk about my school
day · tell the time · name features in a country.

**Unit 3 "Open Day" (book pp.43–60)**. L1 **Welcome to
our Open Day!** (pp.44–45): a reading text by Lina —
"Welcome to our Open Day! In May we have our school
Open Day. Lots of people come to our school. My mum
and dad are coming and my little sister, **Salwa**. My
class has a stall and we are selling cakes. Yousif's
class has a stall and they are selling drinks for the
children and the parents. There are lots of races and
games for everyone. It is very exciting!" — with tick-
or-cross reading checks, the **months** chanted as a
rhyme (January … December), and "What month is it? ·
When is your birthday? — It's in ___. · When is the
next holiday?" L2 **What do you want to drink?**
(pp.46–47): the drinks stall (coffee, orange juice,
lemonade, cola, water, mango juice, tea, milk — choose
five) and the stall dialogs: "What do you want? · Can
I help you? · A cup of tea and some milk, please. ·
Can I have an orange juice, please? · Can I have milk?
· I want a cola. · I want a lemonade." L3 **What do
you want to eat?** (pp.48–49): the food stall —
delicious, watermelon, cakes, sandwiches, pizza —
with prices asked and answered: "How much are the
cakes? — They're 2.5 Libyan Dinars each." and the
Book Map's "Who buys watermelon? What does Khaled
buy?" (**Khaled** shops here). L4 (pp.50–51) is whose
in print, over clothes: "This is Yousif's T-shirt. It's
Yousif's. These are Yousif's trainers. They're
Yousif's." — "Whose is this? — It's Yousif's." L5
**What's wrong?** (pp.52–53): a spot-the-mistake
picture joined up as sentences (Yousif in a hat, scarf
and coat; "Lina's egg is bigger than the spoon.";
**Rana**; **Tariq** with an orange on his spoon; "The
teacher's trousers are **too short**."; "Nadia's
trainers are **too big**.") — and **two emails**: Lina
to **Suzan**: "Hi Suzan / Today was our school Open
Day. I ran in the egg and spoon race. I was first! My
brother, Yousif, was the winner in the running race.
My class had a stall with cakes, cookies and lots of
nice things to eat. Yousif's class had a stall with
drinks. It was fun! / Lina" — and Suzan back: "Hi Lina
/ What cakes did you make? I am good at making
cookies. Can you come to my school Open Day in June?
There is a skipping race and a hopping race! I am in
the blue team. What colour is your team? Here is a
picture of me skipping. / Suzan" — the past tense
living in letters again. L6 **What's first?**
(pp.54–55): the Open Day **programme** with times
(11.00–11.30 The Lion and the Mouse, a play · 11.30–
12.00 Healthy Habits, a song · 12.10–12.25 Spelling
Competition · 12.30–12.45 Music Show), the ordinals
taught as pairs — "1 = one, **1st = first** · 2 = two,
**2nd = second** · 3 = three, **3rd = third** · 4 =
four, **4th = fourth**" — "Who was first? Lina was
first in the race. Tariq was second. Rana was third.
They all got a rosette.", and a marks table (English,
Maths, Science: Yousif 78/60/75 · Kareem 55/65/70 ·
Nadia 80/70/69 · Tariq 63/75/68) talked over: "Nadia
was first in English. — Yes, and Yousif was second."
L7 **Making a Poster** (pp.56–57): a poster frame —
School name (Green Tree School), Day and date
(Saturday, 4th June), Stalls (Cakes and cookies,
drinks), Races (Running race at 2:30 · Skipping race
at 3 o'clock) — "Now make your poster.", read the
others', give prizes (the lesson is marked **P for
Project!**). L8 Story: **Races on the Sports Field**
(pp.58–59), a past-tense race story whose verbs carry
adverbs: "It was a sunny day in May. At school, it was
Open Day and there were races on the sports field. The
first race was a running race. The teacher shouted
**loudly**. 'Ready, steady, go!' Some children ran
**quickly** and some children ran **slowly**. Next, it
was the egg and spoon race. The teacher shouted
loudly. 'Walk **carefully**!' Nadia was in front. She
was winning the race, but she walked too quickly and
dropped her egg. 'Oh, no! My egg!' Lina walked quickly
but carefully. She was first! Tariq was second! 'Well
done! Well done, Lina!' The teacher gave a gold star
to all the children. Everyone was happy and cheered
loudly. Kareem and Lina were first in their races.
Tariq and Rana were second and third in the egg and
spoon race. 'Did you enjoy the races?' 'Yes, we did!
It was great! Yippee!'" "Now I can" (p.60): talk about
school Open Days · name the months · ask for and
offer food and drink · talk about the price of things
· talk about who things belong to · make a poster and
talk about it.

**Unit 4 "Revision (1)" (book pp.61–66)** — four
lessons: review talk and a shop role-play ("Can I help
you? — How much are the cakes? — 2.5 LYD each. — Can I
have 2, please? — Yes, here you are."); months
questions ("How many months in a year? · What month
comes after May? · What month comes before
September?"); **The Magic Bird** — a story the class
completes by choosing words and then retells: "There
was an old man. He lived in a small village next to a
long river. One day, he walked to the river and saw a
very big [bird]. The bird had a [yellow] face and
[three] legs. The old man looked at the bird. The old
man was [sad]. The bird looked at the old man. 'I want
to be [young] again.' 'Can I help you? I give you your
wish!' There was a [green] light. The old man wasn't
there. 'Oh no! The old man is [older] than he wanted
to be!'" — "Tell your story to a partner."; and a
**Snakes and Ladders** board whose squares carry the
three units' words. "Now I can": answer questions
about Units 1–3 · answer questions for a school
challenge · complete and tell a story · play a game
about English.

**Unit 5 "Places to See" (book pp.67–84)**. L1
**Different Places** (pp.68–69): museum, lake, cave,
forest, waterfall, ruin, matched to their describing
words (beautiful, ancient, large, interesting,
famous, high); **The Libyan Museum** information file
("The Libyan Museum is very famous. There are lots of
beautiful and ancient things in the museum. Can you
see the head of an old statue? Find people in a cave
painting. Can you find the ancient stone tablet? Find
the pattern on the mosaic."); and definitions to
match: "A very large hole in the earth. — cave · An
ancient flat square of stone. — tablet · A model of a
person made from stone. — statue · A picture made
with lots of small coloured pieces of glass or stone.
— mosaic". L2 **A School Trip** (pp.70–71): a class
vote ("How many pupils want to see the seaside?") and
the going-to conversation: "We're going to see high
mountains with snow. Then we're going to do a project
about waterfalls. I'm going to take my backpack and
wear boots. — So am I. I'm going to wear boots, a hat
and a scarf." L3 **On the Train** (pp.72–73): what the
children are doing, sentences built from word tiles
("Tariq is sleeping."), the **Rules on the school
trip** — "Sit quietly. · Do not eat your lunch on the
train. · Do not talk loudly. · Do not walk around. ·
Do not forget your hats and bags." — and the train
rhyme: "We're on a school trip / And we're having fun!
/ I'm drawing, she's sleeping, / They're writing, he's
reading, / We're on a big train / And we're having
fun! / I'm singing, she's talking, / They're eating,
he's drinking…" L4 **At the Waterfall** (pp.74–75): a
trip timetable to fill by listening (Arrive at ·
Activities 1st–4th · Lunch at · Train leaves at), the
Waterfall Shop's prices (2–20 LYD), and spending a
budget in going-to sentences: "You have 30 LYD. What
are you going to buy? — I'm going to buy 1 mug, 2
notebooks and a cookie. · I'm going to buy crayons
and a hat." L5 **Mountains in Libya** (pp.76–77): snow,
mountain range, mountain, clouds, and a real
information text: "Nafusa Mountains are in the west
of Libya. They are **968 metres high**. Lots of
interesting animals live in the mountains. There are
also lots of beautiful plants and trees. The weather
is different to other places in Libya, **because** the
mountains are very high. In the winter, it is very
cold and sometimes snowy. In the summer, it is mild
and dry." — then **Class 4's mini project about the
Ubari Oasis**, printed with mistakes in its black
words for the class to find ("The Ubari Oasis lies
between the Sahara **dessert** and the very **young**
sand dunes of the Idehan Ubari desert. The dunes are
more than 100,000 years old! … a beautiful oasis with
**sand** in it. There are 20 small salt lakes
**between** the oasis."), and "Write Class 4's mini
project again and draw pictures. Try to get a red
rosette!" L6 **Higher and Highest** (pp.78–79): the
three steps taught on pictures — high / higher /
**highest**, cold / colder / **coldest**: "A is high.
B is higher. C is the highest." — then judgment
sentences said and written Yes or No ("A cow is
bigger than a cat. · A tree is taller than a man. · An
elephant, a monkey and a crocodile – a crocodile is
the biggest. (No)"). L7 **Let's play a game!**
(pp.80–81): partners score points for true comparison
sentences ("The red apple is bigger than the yellow
apple. — Yes, it is. 1 point." · "The green apple is
the biggest. — Good! 1 point for you."), over the
three-form table (big/bigger/biggest · small/smaller/
smallest · long/longer/longest · short/shorter/
shortest). L8 **A Trip to the Museum** (pp.82–83):
the trip told in going-to lines ("And we're going to
go on a train. We're going to have a picnic. We're
going to see high mountains with snow.").

**Unit 6 "In the Past" (book pp.85–102)** — the past
tense's own unit. L1 **Wh- Questions** (pp.86–87): the
six question words matched to what they ask — who =
a person · what = a thing or an animal · when = a
time · where = a place · how = a number, or the way
we do something · **why = means because** — questions
matched to their answers ("How do you go to school? —
By bus. · Why are you wearing a hat? — Because it is
cold. · Where is the library? — It's next to the
school. · When do you wake up? — At 7 o'clock. · Who
is the tall man? — He is my brother. · What are these?
— They're sweets."), and a build-a-question practice.
L2 **Yesterday** (pp.88–89): first a **make** — a
days-of-the-week chart built by instructions in the
sequence words ("First, draw a rectangle and draw 7
lines. You need: Paper, Crayons, Scissors, Ruler.
Then, write 'Days of the week' in the top rectangle.
Next, write 'Sunday' in the second rectangle. After
that, write all the other days. Then make 2 arrows
and a star. Next, write yesterday, today and
tomorrow. Finally, put the arrows and the star next
to the days of your week."); then **Muna's yesterday**
in four parts to put in order ("Yesterday, Muna went
to school on the bus. Her first lesson was maths.
After PE, it was break time. Muna played with her
friends. They climbed up the ladder and went down the
slide. Her next lessons were English and science. For
lunch she had a sandwich and juice. At 2 o'clock it
was time to go home. At home, Muna played with her
friends and did her homework. She went to bed at 8
o'clock.") — Muna, Grade 2's lead girl, is the example
— and the tense taught as **pairs**: "I go to school.
Yesterday, I **went** to school. · My first lesson is
maths. Yesterday, my first lesson **was** maths. · I
play with my friends. Yesterday, I **played** with my
friends. · I do my homework. Yesterday, I **did** my
homework." L3 **Question Game** (pp.90–91): a
story-building game on six spinners — When? (Yesterday
/ On Monday / On Friday / On Sunday) · Who? (a boy / a
girl / a monkey / a mouse / a cat) · How? (quickly /
slowly / loudly / carefully) · What? (ate an ice-cream
/ ate some cheese / made a cake / drank some milk /
ate a banana) · Where? (in the classroom / in the
kitchen / in the garden / in the zoo / in the living
room) · Why? (because he/she/it was hungry / thirsty)
— one built example: "On Monday, a cat slowly ate an
ice-cream in the kitchen because it was hungry!"
L4 **Nadia's Grandma** (pp.92–93): "Grandma, tell me
about when you were 9. — When I was 9 … I was tall. I
had long, black hair. — You were pretty. — I played
skipping at school. — You were good at skipping. — I
always did my homework. — You were a good pupil." —
and grandpa read about: "When he was 10, Nadia's
grandpa had short, brown hair. He was a good pupil
and he always did his homework. He played football at
school with his friends. He was good at football." —
then the pair role-play, one as grandpa with prompt
cards (tall · short, brown hair · strong · football ·
good at football · at school). L5 **At the Cinema**
(pp.94–95): cinema words — film, funny, popcorn,
robot, ice-cream — and talk about a film visit in the
past. L6 **Where did you go?** (pp.96–97): the
question rhyme in full — "Where did you go? / Where
did you go? / I went to the zoo. / What did you see? /
I saw elephants, and monkeys, too. / Who did you go
with? / I went with Ali. / When did you go there? / We
went at three. / How did you get there? / We went in
Ali's car. / Why did you go there? / Because it was
my birthday!" — and a cloze text of Yousif's yesterday
at the playground, its verbs chosen from went, was,
had, played, climbed, walked, crawled. L7 **There was
… / There were …** (pp.98–99): a memory game — look
for 30 seconds, cover the pictures, and make sentences
for points — with the Let's check! box: "There **was
a** book. / There **were** books. / There **was an**
apple. / There **were** apples." L8 Story: **The
Little Brown Bird** (pp.100–101) — why the robin has
a red breast, in full past tense: "Many years ago,
there was a little brown bird that lived at the top
of a tall, tall tree. It was a happy little bird and
it sang loudly and its song was beautiful. Winter
came, the snow came and a cold wind blew. It got
colder and colder. A father and son were travelling a
long way to their home. They had nowhere to stay. The
father made a small fire with sticks and both went to
sleep on the cold ground. The fire started to go out.
A hungry wolf saw the man and boy. 'A tasty meal for
me today! Yum, yum!' The little bird saw the wolf and
saw the fire. 'I must help. I must make the fire burn
again.' The little bird flapped its wings and the
fire got bigger and bigger, but the fire burnt the
little bird's breast. The man and boy woke up and saw
the brave little bird. They thanked the little bird
loudly. 'You have saved us from the wolf!' From that
day, these little brown birds have a red breast.
Brave little birds!" "Now I can" (p.102): ask and
answer Wh- questions · say what I did yesterday ·
talk about how people looked and what they did in
the past · talk about the cinema · say where I went
· use There was … and There were ….

**Unit 7 "The Challenge" (book pp.103–120)**. L1
**The School Challenge** (pp.104–105): an invitation
email read — Red River School to Green Tree School:
four challenges (sports, English, maths, art), "11
o'clock on Tuesday, 4th April", by bus, "1st 2nd 3rd
rosettes", the School Challenge Cup — answered with
the Wh- questions (What is it? When is it? Where is
it? How are we going to get there? What are the
challenges?), a Remember box of the Open Day results,
a challenge ticked for each child, and going-to
sentences to finish ("Kareem is going to do the ___
challenge."). L2 **You need a ruler** (pp.106–107):
the four challenges spelled out — Maths: "Draw a
square, a triangle, a rectangle. The teacher will
tell you how big or small." · Sports: "Running 100
metres. Ball and bucket race." · English · Art — and
what each child has and needs. L3 (pp.108–109):
**Have you got …?** practised over two children's
things (ruler, T-shirt, rubber, ball, trainers,
pencil, picture dictionary, crayons) — "Have you got
a ruler? — Yes, I have. / No, I haven't." — and "Can I
borrow yours? — Yes, here you are." L4 **must /
mustn't** (pp.110–111): the children's own poster
rules, judged Yes or No: "I mustn't talk in a test. ·
I must drink lemonade. (No) · I must clean my teeth. ·
I mustn't listen to my teacher. (No) · I must help
Lina and my family. · I mustn't shout in class. · I
must eat cookies every day. (No) · I must pray." —
then **make your poster** (green ticks for must, red
crosses for mustn't, happy and sad faces; "You need:
Paper, Crayons, Pencils, Pens") and present it: "This
is my poster. First, I must help my mum and dad. —
Yes, we must help them." L5 **Challenge Rules**
(pp.112–113): the teacher's rule chain in order —
"First, you must put on your badges. Next, you must
write out your forms. Now, you must find your chair.
Remember! You mustn't talk. Remember! You mustn't
look at your friend's answers! Now, sports pupils
follow me to the playground." — Kareem's **Challenge
form** filled in (Name: Kareem · Age: 9 · School:
Green Tree School · Challenge: Sports · I need: a
T-shirt, a small ball, some trainers), and the child
writes their own. L6 **Maths and Art** (pp.114–115):
Tariq's maths challenge judged with a ruler — "1 Draw
a rectangle – **4 cm x 2 cm**. 2 Draw a square – **3
cm x 3 cm**. 3 Draw a triangle – your own size." (3
points for each shape) — and Lina's art challenge: a
picture drawn from words alone — "There is a high
mountain and a long river between green fields. In
the fields there are trees and some cows." (2 points
for each correct drawing). L7 **English and Sport**
(pp.116–117): Nadia's English challenge judged —
matching and spelling (river printed "r i v r" among
mountain, waterfall, lake — "What is wrong? Give
Nadia a score from 1 to 9.") — and Kareem's ball-and-
bucket race counted and scored. L8 Story: **The Big
Bike Race** (pp.118–119): "It is summer and it's time
for the big bike race. Everyone is going to ride in
the race. It's a long race. Everyone is doing
exercises. 'Has everyone got a bike?' 'I haven't.'
Kareem hasn't got a bike, but his brother has and he
is going to borrow it. 'My brother is taller than me,
but I can ride his bike.' 'I don't like riding. You
can borrow my bike.' 'What do the children need for
the long race? It is going to be hot.' 'We need
water.' 'We need hats.'" — the parents' rules: "You
must help your friends. · You must ride carefully. ·
You mustn't go too fast." — "Kareem is in front.
Yousif is behind him. The other children are slower.
… 'Oh no! Kareem has fallen off.' 'It was a stone.'
Yousif helps him. 'Are you ok?' 'Quickly! Let's go.'
'It's the finish. Who is first? It's Kareem and
Yousif together! They are both first!'" "Now I can"
(p.120): talk about a school challenge · ask and
answer about it · decide what I have and what I need
· use must/mustn't for rules · fill in a challenge
form · ask and answer about the maths, art, English
and sport challenges.

**Unit 8 "Revision (2)" (book pp.121–126)** — four
lessons: L1 **Test your partner** (p.122): partners
quiz each other with the year's question words. L2
**My Progress** (p.123): the child checks their own
progress — the year's only self-marking page. L3
**Ice-creams for everyone!** (p.124): a cloze story to
complete and retell — "Yesterday, we saw an old lady
crossing a busy road. 'Can you help me?' The children
helped the old lady to cross the road. 'You must speak
loudly, but you mustn't shout. First look left, then
look right.' They crossed the road quickly but safely.
They were next to an ice-cream shop. 'Thank you! Do
you like ice-cream?' … 'I'm going to buy ice-creams
for us!' Lina wanted a mango ice-cream. Yousif wanted
a chocolate one. … 'We're going to come every week
and have ice-cream!'" L4 **A Pronunciation Game**
(p.125): a board game whose squares ask the year's
sound questions — "What sound does 'ph' make? · Say
a word that has 'ph'. · What sound does 'igh' make? ·
Say 'high'. · How do you say '-ly' (quickly)? · Spell
a word with 'ee'. · What sound does 'sh' make? · What
sound does 'ch' make?" "Now I can": answer questions
about Units 5–7 · ask questions to test my partner ·
complete and tell a story · play a game about
English. The book ends with a **Word list** (pp.127–
137): every unit's words with an example sentence for
each — its Unit 1 entry for *grade* reads "I'm in
Grade 4 at school.", which is the inside-grade note
at the head of this section.

→ Filed/recorded from this book: comparatives and
superlatives (longer than / the biggest) with their
opposites table; whose + 's answers; countries,
capitals and the Libya profile; hundreds to 1,000;
minutes in fives and 3.30 time; months and ordinals;
prices in Libyan Dinars; going-to; the past tense as
present/past pairs with went/was/did; There was/were;
the Wh- set with why = because; Have you got…?;
must/mustn't; draw-to-size shapes in centimetres; and
the year's stories (Lion and Mouse, City Mouse,
Sports Field races, Little Brown Bird, Big Bike Race)
— held here for the Word, Sentence, Paragraph and
Maths titles. Acceptance counts unchanged — same
country, a new grade's spiral.

**Libya Grade 5 Pupils Book — READ COMPLETE
2026-10-25.**

### Grade 5 Activity Book — unit by unit

The writing twin, in black and white, and the most
teaching-dense of the year's three books: nearly every
Pupils lesson is re-taught here in writing, and several
things exist **only** in this book. Exercises carry a
**C21 SKILLS** badge where they practise a named
21st-century skill. Audio runs to at least Track 146.

**Unit 1 (book pp.4–19).** Write about yourself from
Nadia's model ("My name is Nadia. I'm 9. I live with
my mum, dad and three brothers.") on Name/Age/Live
lines; sentence-half matching; a **blends wheel** (bl,
fl, pl, sl) completing black, plane, flower, sleep,
plant and **flag** — the picture is a Libyan flag;
Lina's text copied as a frame ("Hello, I'm ___");
playground words defined in writing ("You crawl
through this. What is it? It's a ___." ×6) with a
self-score line ("How many points did you get? I got
___ points."); a class like/dislike survey grid ("Do
you like the slide? — Yes, I do. / No, I don't.");
movement words matched to arrow pictures (down,
along, up, through, around); **Our classroom rules**
judged tick or cross by sense ("Do stand on tables.
✗ · Do put rubbish in the bin. ✓ · Do not open your
books. ✗") and two rules of your own built from word
tiles into a **My rules** box; blends heard and
pointed (cl, cr, pl, st, sl, gr, sw) in two-blend
phrases ("**pl**aying in the **pl**ayground · **sw**eeping
under the **sw**ing · I'm **gr**eat in **gr**ade 4!");
**contractions taught as "short words"** — "Look and
think about the rule. Let's check! do not = don't" —
I am = I'm · He is · She is · It is · It is not = It
isn't · He can not = He can't · There is · There is
not = There isn't · What is = What's; the bag game in
print ("Is there a ruler in your bag? — Yes, there
is. / No, there isn't. · Are there any …? — Yes, there
are. / No, there aren't."); opposite pairs under
pictures; "Think of a word to match" (A big ___ — "You
can look in your Pupil's Book for ideas!"); comparative
sentences unscrambled from tiles; **the tens traced
as dotted words** (ten…ninety, 100 "a hundred") and
two-part numbers matched (thirty-two = 32 …);
the full week timetable as a table (Sunday-first;
art, science, maths, RE, Arabic, music, English, PE,
with a Break row) asked and answered ("What's on
Tuesday? — First, it's maths. Then …") and your own
written for two days; whose matching + eight written
"It's ___'s" answers; choose your shop's stock from
four lists and draw it on top/middle/bottom shelves —
then listen to your partner and draw **their** shelves
("My cookies are on the top shelf. — OK."); the
**Look, say, cover, write, check** grid printed as a
worksheet (Unit 1 words: here, there, than, whose —
the grid returns every unit: U2 from/about/that/say ·
U3 our/your/very/want · U5 now/many/much/their · U6
why/when/was/were · U7 must/mustn't/any/need); a
numbers listening grid (11–100 with the words printed
under the figures); and The Lion and the Mouse closed-
book work ("Close your books. Who said it?" — "You
are smaller than me." Lion · "I must help my friend."
Mouse).

**Unit 2 (book pp.20–35).** Where's he/she from? /
Where are they from? with He's/She's/They're from…,
found and written over the map children (Brazil,
England, Libya, Russia, China); a flag-colour guessing
game with books closed ("It's yellow. — I know! It's
Russia."); **capital letters sorted by kind** — "Look!
These names have capital letters." — names of
countries / people / languages sorted, then monday,
arabic, friday, china, kareem rewritten with capitals;
the Adel–Sam dialog as a cloze, checked against the
Pupil's Book; write about yourself by four questions
(name / from / live / languages); an **Information
file LIBYA** with a real photograph of Tripoli
("Capital city – Tripoli · Language – Arabic ·
Weather – hot and dry") answered in writing; a second
file, **Libya vs Russia** (summer 30°C/25°C, winter
19°C/**−10°C**) with homework judgments ("Russia is
smaller than Libya. No · Winter in Russia is colder.
Yes"); numbers written in order across tens and
hundreds; a listen-and-cross number game (Pupil A/B
grids); **Yousif's school day** read ("Today is
Monday. I go to school at 7 o'clock. I go to school
by bus. There are 1,000 children in my school… I have
my lunch at home with my mum and my sister.") and
your own written in its frame with picture boxes to
draw (clock, bus, sum, music, family, A B C); a **th**
wheel (bathroom, thousand, thumb, teeth, think,
three) with cover-and-write; **nine clocks** with the
times written in words ("What's the time? — It's …");
the **Can you help me? game** — partners answer in
different modes: spell it (letters on dashes), draw
it, write the digits, say it; the landscape picture
numbered (mountains, village, desert, lake, stream,
field, oil field, city, river) + a compass rose
completed (North/East/South/West); adjective cloze
over the same scene; the four Pupil's-Book riddles
written out in full (mountain, desert, river, city)
and **write a puzzle of your own** from a word bank;
landscape words unscrambled; City Mouse sorted into
a City/Village table (quiet, rich food, scary cat,
simple food, big house, delicious food) with "Which
mouse do you like?"; **circle the odd word** (Libya/
Tripoli/China/America — Tripoli is a city · Arabic/
lunch/English/maths …) and name-the-groups.

**Unit 3 (book pp.36–51).** Lina's Open Day text as a
cloze; word groups sorted (Numbers / People /
Countries); the months grid completed from the rhyme
with self-check against the Pupil's Book, and the
birthday dialog in full ("When is your birthday? —
My birthday is in September. — It's in July.");
**The Yummy Stall menu** — a full priced menu read
for listening marks: sandwiches (cheese, chicken,
tomato, jam) 12 LYD · pizza (cheese and olives) 20
LYD · cakes (lemon, chocolate, pear) 3 LYD · cookies
(chocolate, orange, apple, date) 2 LYD · sweets 3 LYD
· watermelon 5 LYD · juice (orange, mango, apple) 8
LYD · milk 6 LYD · cola 7 LYD · lemonade 7 LYD · tea
3 LYD · coffee 5 LYD — then write your own order with
prices and a **Total LYD** (real adding in English);
the alphabet in capitals, cover-and-complete,
make-your-own alphabet puzzle for a partner;
**alphabetical order** — food words sorted by their
underlined first letters, two lists judged Yes/No,
then written in correct order, and **The alphabet
order challenge** (partners write 5 stall words and
swap to order them); "What's wrong? Spell the words
correctly." (cokies, mlik, tee, piza, wata, swets);
**Read. Then cover and remember.** — a races
announcement ("It's now 4 o'clock… Team A is the Red
Team and Team B is the Green Team. The first race is
skipping. The second race is the bike race. Good
luck!" — *recorded difference: the Pupil's Book word
list says "Team A is the Yellow Team"*) answered from
memory and partner-checked; Whose is this? / Whose
are these? over clothes photos with "It is Yousif's
shirt." unscrambles; missing-letters sentences ("The
m**oo**n is in the sk**y** · **Th**e teacher's
tr**ous**ers are too short"); **sh / ch / ph** — "What
sound do these letters make together?" — words you
know written in three columns (shop / chair /
elephant) and a check bank sorted (shirt, cheese,
sharpener, shark, watch, shoes, shoulders, chicken,
alphabet, phone, photo, fish, shelf, wash, brush);
ordinal balloons out of order (3rd 1st 4th 2nd) and
**the test-marks table in full** (Arabic/RE/Art —
Yousif 95/79/70 · Kareem 90/68/82 · Nadia 87/80/89 ·
Rana 77/88/91 · Tariq 65/90/76) with Yes/No listening
and sentences completed from it; **Looking at
syllables** — "A syllable is a small part of a word."
— clap and sort 1/2/3 syllables (mum, dad · sister,
classroom · Nadia, holiday), a bank to sort
(triangle, pencil, hand, eye, horse, lemonade,
exercise, garden, school, frog, crocodile, library),
your own 3 words counted, and test your partner by
clapping; a **Snowy Mountain School poster** completed
and **given a prize** — choose a 1st/2nd/3rd/4th
rosette for it; **Help Adel to write the email**
("To: Sam · Subject: Our Race Day" — "Today was our
school **Race** Day. I was in the **green** team. I
ran in the **jumping** race. I was **third**… It was
**great**!"); the story's adverbs hunted with their
verbs ("What verbs come before each one?" — shouted
loudly …), photos labelled (baby = carefully,
runners = quickly, tortoise = slowly), and
**-teen vs -ty** set side by side with stress marks
(thirteen 13 / thirty 30 … nineteen 19 / ninety 90)
and heard numbers written into teen/ty columns.

**Unit 4 Revision (1) (book pp.52–55)** — only four
lessons (the Pupil's Book's six pages likewise).
**Make your own Bigger and smaller rhyme**: choose
words from six boxes to fill the Pupil's rhyme's frame
and name it ("My rhyme is called: ___"); **Check your
progress!** — Adel's playground email cloze (climbed
**up** · went **down** · crawled **through**), the
Playground Rules unscrambled with full stops ("the
swing not do stand on" → "Do not stand on the
swing."), three about-you answers, adjective–noun
matching; **Kareem's homework "About Libya"** — a
whole text printed with capital-letter mistakes to
circle and correct ("I live in **b**enghazi. The
capital of **l**ibya is **t**ripoli… In **m**arch it
is rainy."), whose facts as printed are: "I'm from
Libya. I live in Benghazi. I live with my mum, dad,
brothers and sisters. The capital of Libya is
Tripoli. The population is about 1.2 million. We
speak Arabic and I am learning English at school. I
like English, but my favourite subjects are PE and
maths. The weather in Libya is usually hot and dry.
In March it is rainy. In summer the temperature is
about 40°C or hotter."; and **The Wrong Shoes** — a
six-panel comic story told in full: "It is Wednesday
morning. It's 8.30 and Sam is late for school. School
starts at 8 o'clock. He gets dressed quickly. He puts
on trousers, a shirt and some shoes. He runs to
school. At school the teacher points to the school
rules on the wall. Sam's friends laugh. His trousers
are shorter than his legs. His sleeves are longer
than his arms. Sam's friends point to his shoes. His
shoes are bigger than his feet! The teacher tells Sam
to go home again and change his clothes. Sam is happy
now." — with nine speech bubbles to number into the
panels ("I'm late for school! · You're wearing your
dad's shoes! · Don't be late!").

**Unit 5 (book pp.56–71).** The describing words in
sentences, then reused from first letters ("The
**ancient** pyramids are in Egypt." · "The waterfall
is 500 metres **high**."); spelling aloud ("How do
you spell 'ancient'? — I know, it's _ n c i _ n _.");
paired definition cards swapped with a partner
(Pupil A: museum, lake, cave, forest — Pupil B: boat,
pencil, waterfall, ruin); **good / better / best**
taught as its own set with ticks: "I think vanilla
ice-cream is **good**. ✓ Strawberry ice-cream is
**better**. ✓✓ But chocolate ice-cream is the
**best**! ✓✓✓"; a **bar chart read** for answers
(slide 25, swing 20, roundabout 15, bars 10, tunnel
5 — "What do they like best?"); a ticks table (5
children × 4 fruits) counted, **drawn and coloured as
your own bar chart**, and answered from ("What fruit
does Lina like best?"); a seaside holiday text ("In
July, we're going to go on holiday to the seaside…
we're going to stay with my aunt and uncle and my
cousins.") with a picture tick-list; the going-to
conversation as a cloze chain (plane · boat · fish ·
play · cousins · sandals · swim); zoo animals matched
and **The zoo timetable** in full ("Arrive at: 2.45 ·
Tea at: 4 o'clock · Bus leaves at: 5.30 · Activities
1st See the elephants · 2nd See the crocodiles · 3rd
See the monkeys · 4th Your own choice"); **The Zoo
Shop!** photo cards (apple juice, candyfloss,
popcorn, hot dogs, kebab, pizza) with prices heard
and written in LYD and totalled; **Let's learn! —
the Nile**: "The Nile is the longest river in the
world. It is about **6,853 kilometres** long. It
flows through many countries in Africa. The picture
is the Nile in Egypt." — a real photo and map,
unscrambles (Nile, Africa, Egypt) and a water cloze
("Water is **wet**. · **Fish** live in rivers. ·
Boats **float** on water… · Small **streams** flow
into rivers. · Rivers flow into the **sea**. ·
Rivers in high mountain places have **waterfalls**.");
Ubari Oasis questions written from the Pupil's
information; superlatives with stress marks
(biggest, smallest, longest, oldest, coldest,
hottest) and **"Around the world by Class 4"** — a
class magazine page with real photos: "Look at the
longest crocodile in the world. He is 110 years old!
He lives in Australia. · **The hottest place on
record in Libya is El Azizia. On 13th September, 1922
it was 58°C.** · The oldest elephant was 86. He lived
in Asia." — then the child's own page, **"Animals
around the world by ___"**, its facts read off photo
labels: "The biggest shark in the world is the
**whale shark**. It is about **13** metres long. ·
The smallest bird in the world is the **bee
hummingbird**. It is only **57** millimetres long.
**Measure that with your ruler.** · The hottest
desert in the world is the **Sahara**. It is in
**Africa**. It is **58**°C."; superlatives unscrambled;
odd-word rows; name-the-groups; **A Trip to the
Museum ordered from ten sentences** — which gives the
story's whole plot: get up · breakfast · bus ·
timetable · rules · "Rami is missing and Suzan
cries." · "Rami comes back and he is carrying a
lion's head." · lunch in the museum café · "The
balloon goes pop. Everyone laughs." · presents in the
museum shop; and **ee / ea / y — one sound**: the
letters underlined in 20 words, the box "The sound
is the same for **ee**, **ea** and **y** in these
words", five chant lines ("A hungry bee · A tall
tree with green leaves · I see the green sea · My
teacher eats sweets · Baby please sleep quickly"),
and the warning "Some **ea** words make different
sounds: breakfast, wear, ear."

**Unit 6 (book pp.72–87)** — the past tense's writing
twin, and the strongest grammar teaching in the year.
The Wh- words' **sounds** sorted first: what/when/
where/why say /w/, who/how/whose say /h/; question
words written for picture-cued questions ("**How** old
is she? · **Whose** boots are these?"); answers
matched; **Punctuation taught by name** — match (. =
full stop · ? = question mark · A B C D = capital
letters) and colour them in three teaching sentences:
"A capital letter starts a sentence. · A full stop
ends a sentence. · What is a question mark? It's a
full stop for a question sentence."; the days table
(Sunday-first) with three-tense questions ("What day
is it today? It's ___. · What day was it yesterday?
It was ___. · What day will it be tomorrow? It will
be ___."); a days rhyme completed with your own days
("The days of the week / go quickly by! / Time goes
by, time does fly!"); "Yesterday, I …" sentences
matched to pictures — two of them planted swaps
(washing face vs "cleaned my teeth") — and the -ed
set said aloud; **-ed sorted by sound: /d/ · /t/ ·
/id/** — underline the -ed in 17 verbs, hear each
one and write it under its sound (played /d/ ·
washed /t/ · visited /id/), colouring the shapes;
the **LOOK box states the rule in words**: "These
are **verbs** in the **past**. They are **regular**
verbs – they end in **-ed**. play + ed = played ·
live + d = lived"; circle the correct verb in eight
Yesterday sentences; write two of your own;
**irregular named as irregular** — "Can we say
'goed'? Yesterday, I goed to school. — Why? — **No,
we can't! Because this verb is irregular. We say
'went'.**" — and R or I written for 18 pairs (go/
went · am, are/was, were · get/got · see/saw · have/
had · make/made · do/did · eat/ate · swim/swam ·
buy/bought irregular; watch, clean, play, look,
start, talk, listen to, stay regular); 30 mixed verbs
numbered into present/past pairs (give/gave shown;
**learn/learnt** among them); the partner game
("Have. — Had. — Yes, that's correct."); **Liz's
night story** built entirely from past verbs: "My
name is Liz. Yesterday, at night, I **heard** a
noise. I **got** out of bed and **went** to the
kitchen. There was nothing! Then, I **went** to the
bathroom. There was nothing! Then, I **went** to the
living room. What did I see? I **saw** my baby sister
sitting on the floor. She **had** a big cake in her
hand. She **looked** at me and smiled! She **said**
that she was hungry. She **ate** all the cake. We
went to the bathroom. She **cleaned** her teeth. We
went back to bed!" — retold to a partner; question-
word anatomy (underline the words that answer the
question word, then write the missing question words
into past questions — "**What time** did your baby
sister go to bed?"); a **past-tense board game** — 26
verb circles (say…give) with Miss a turn! squares and
a spinner: "Congratulations! You know your past
tense!"; **Nadia's email "My weekend"** with its past
verbs fitted (went, stayed, was, swam, bought, had,
ate, played, arrived) and four false sentences to
correct against it ("Nadia travelled by car." →
"Nadia travelled by bus."); a wordsearch of 12 past
verbs (took, stayed, swam, ate, played, bought, went,
did, said, saw, washed, had); **Lina's day** — 14
past sentences read, counted and numbered in order
("I took a bus. I stayed with my grandma. I swam in
the sea. I ate fish. I played football. I bought an
ice-cream. I went to the park. I did my homework. I
said hello to my friends. I saw a fun park. I washed
my hands. I had my lunch. I was tired. I went to
bed!") and your own day told in the same frames;
Libya's weather by month (January mild · March cold
and rainy · August hot and sunny); and **nouns and
adjectives defined** in a Let's check! box — "•
**Nouns** are the names of things. • **Adjectives**
tell us about the things – are they big, small,
happy, sad? • Adjectives and nouns go together." —
hunted in The Little Brown Bird and matched
(beautiful song · tasty meal · cold ground · small
fire · brave bird · hungry wolf · red breast · tall
tree), closing with six category boxes to fill with
four words each (Countries · Irregular past verbs ·
Sports/games · Places · Regular past verbs ·
Adjectives).

**Unit 7 (book pp.88–103).** The invitation as a
choose-the-correct-word letter — *recorded
difference: this letter gives the challenges as
"science, maths, Arabic and English", the day as
"Wednesday, 10th October" and the start "at 2
o'clock", where the Pupil's Book's telling has
sports/English/maths/art on 4th April at 11* — then
the questions written for given answers; alphabet
order by pairs; **What do you need?** — four
situations matched to their needs (a friend's
birthday, 15th March, turning 10 → a card and a
present · the school's 100 metre race in July, very
hot → trainers, T-shirt, running trousers, a hat and
some water · a seaside holiday in August → sandals
and swimming things · a school trip to the mountains
→ some money for presents); the months completed
from their endings; **-igh and -y make the sound of
'I'** ("The sound is like **I**. -igh and sometimes y
make the sound the same as 'I'.") with two sentences
written from a word bank for two photos; **big
numbers in full words** — nine written-out numbers
found in a digit grid ("Five hundred and fifty-
eight" … "One thousand") and figures written as
words up to **2,000** (525 = "five hundred and
twenty-five" shown); two borrowing conversations
completed and ordered ("Have you got a **football**?
— No, I haven't, but I need one for **school** today.
Can I borrow yours? — Yes, here you are. — Thank
you."); **the school library rules** on a noticeboard
— "1 Do not shout. 2 Do not run around. 3 Talk
quietly. 4 Do not eat. 5 Do not drink. 6 Do not write
in the books. 7 Take care of the books. 8 Do not take
books home." — re-said by Nadia and Kareem with must/
mustn't ("The first one says we **mustn't shout**…
Number 3 says we **must talk quietly**."), matched
to six no-signs, and rewritten from memory under
"Let's check! must not = mustn't"; **P for Project!
— make flags**, as a play-script ("Kareem: Our Libya
flag. Nadia: Yes, and our school flag. Tariq: And
let's make the Red River School flag too.") and a
**Let's make!** page ("You need: a big piece of
paper, a pencil, a ruler, crayons, some glue, a
stick" — draw the flag, colour it and write the
words, put it on a long stick, "Take your flag to
the School Challenge!"); eight sentences unscrambled
with the capitals deliberately wrong in the jumble
("Remember the capital letters!" — "need do I for
maths challenge the What?" → "What do I need for the
maths challenge?" · "haven't I got a dictionary
picture." → "I haven't got a picture dictionary.");
twelve must/mustn't rule statements judged ✓/✗
against the Pupil's challenge pages; **a strength-of-
opinion scale** — ticks read as VERY VERY VERY VERY /
VERY VERY VERY / VERY VERY / NOT VERY / NO — marked
by Me and My partner against ten life rules ("I must
help my family. · I must pray. · I must eat healthy
food. · I must take exercise every day. · I must do
my homework. · I must keep my bedroom tidy. · I must
shout in class. ✗"); the eight library rules written
as must/mustn't sentences ("You mustn't shout in the
library."); The Big Bike Race checked by ten tick
answers with traps ("Lina hasn't got a bike. No ·
Is Kareem going to borrow his dad's bike? No, he
isn't. · Kareem is the winner and Yousif is second.
No"); and the story's own lines as choose-the-word
bubbles ("I **don't** like riding… · You must ride
**carefully**. · You **mustn't** go too fast. · It
was a **stone**.")

**Unit 8 Revision (2) (book pp.104–107)** + back
matter. Months built from syllable tiles; **write
the punctuation** — seven bare sentences rewritten
with capitals, full stops and question marks ("when
is your birthday" → "When is your birthday?" · "in
libya march is a rainy month" · "it's in april");
comparison statements judged true or false ("An
elephant is taller than a giraffe. ✗ · The Nile is
the longest river in the world. ✓"); **Check my
progress!** — Adel's School Challenge email cloze
("It is **on** 4th April… There **is** a School
Challenge Cup for the winners. It is **big** and is
made of gold!"); **"Read about you"** — a past-tense
text written as if about the reader ("Yesterday, I
went shopping. I went with my older brother and my
friend… We saw a film about the fastest car in the
world. We ate ice-cream and drank lemonade… It was a
great day!") answered with six past Wh- questions in
full sentences; **the "I can say it in English!"
game** — a 26-space wheel, every space a speaking
task from the year ("Say the numbers 8, 18 and 80 ·
Say the capital of Libya · Say the past of **brush**
· Say the past of **have** · Say something you
**mustn't** do · Say what you need for a **bike
race** · Say the name of the highest mountain …");
and **The Goodbye Song**, the year's farewell, its
ten verses a cloze that reviews the whole year —
and its first line settles this book's inside grade
in the book's own words: "**It's time to go, we did
Grade 4, / In Grade 5, we're going to learn more!**"
("We know a bus is bigger than a van, / And we can
spell beautiful, yes we can! · … A desert is dry and
a mountain is high, / Both of the words sound the
same as why! · … The past of do is did, and eat is
ate, / I went to bed early, I mustn't be late. · …
Tomorrow is the day for which we pray, / We're going
to work and we're going to play! / Hurray! Hurray!").
The book closes with **Spelling Tests** (book p.108 —
blank four-word grids per unit; as printed, Unit 3's
grid appears twice and Unit 1 has none), the
**Picture/Word Cards** (pp.109–116 — the year's words
in alphabetical order, along…down on the first page,
with their picture halves) and the **Game Circles**
(pp.117–121 — the 1–6 number spinners for the board
games).

→ Filed/recorded from this book: contractions as
short words; alphabetical order as a taught skill;
syllables defined and clapped; punctuation named
(full stop, question mark, capital letter); regular
-ed with its three sounds /d/ /t/ /id/ and the +d
spelling case; irregular verbs named, with the
goed→went correction dialog; nouns and adjectives
defined; good/better/best; numbers written in full
words to 2,000; bar charts read and drawn; the Nile
at 6,853 km; El Azizia 58°C (13 Sep 1922); whale
shark 13 m; bee hummingbird 57 mm; the Yummy Stall
and Zoo Shop price lists; The Wrong Shoes and Liz's
night story in full; and the Goodbye Song's own
statement that this course year is the children's
Grade 4 going into Grade 5. Acceptance counts
unchanged — same country, the same grade's twin book.

**Libya Grade 5 Activity Book — READ COMPLETE
2026-10-25.**

### Grade 5 Teacher Book — what it adds

Audio runs to **Track 149**. The Introduction settles
this level's grade in the publisher's own words:
**"This level of 21st Century English for Libya has
been developed for teaching English to children in
the fourth year of primary."** — while calling the
level "Primary 5" throughout. So the cover-vs-inside
gap found in the Pupils and Activity books is the
course's own design: the books labelled Primary 5
teach the fourth year. (Filed under the library's
Grade 5, as shelved, with this note standing.)

- The year's **seven stories** are named: The Lion
  and the Mouse, Races on the Sports Field, The
  Magic Bird, A Trip to the Museum, The Little Brown
  Bird, The Big Bike Race and Ice-cream for everyone!
  (the last title printed here with "Ice-cream"
  singular; the Pupils Book prints "Ice-creams for
  everyone!").
- **Four main characters**: Yousif, Lina, Nadia and
  Kareem (Salwa, Rana, Tariq and the rest are the
  wider cast).
- **Assessment changes at this level**, in the book's
  words: "There was no formal assessment in the early
  primary grades. This occurs for the first time in
  Primary 5." — with progress charts kept by the
  teacher across confidence, interest, interaction and
  behaviour, and the spelling tests now real (the AB
  Spelling Tests page, p.108, is worked from).
- The classroom-language list stands as in the
  earlier years (Sit down, please. … "Can I have a
  pencil, please?"), now with wall signs of learner
  phrases added: "Pardon? I don't understand. What
  does … mean? Can you say that again, please? How
  do you spell …? Can I borrow a pen?"
- Two printed slips kept as printed: the aims list
  says "consolildate the English alphabet" and
  "consolildate the sound of English" (for
  consolidate).
- **A Trip to the Museum — the full telling (Track
  94)**, titled here "Rami and the School Trip", kept
  word for word: "1. Suzan and Rami are excited this
  morning. They're going on a school trip. They are
  going to a museum. They're going on a big bus.
  2. They get up at 6.30. They have breakfast. They
  have eggs, bread, cheese and apple juice. Their
  backpacks are by the door. 3. The bus comes.
  Everyone jumps on the bus. The teacher talks about
  the rules on the school trip. Teacher: Sit quietly.
  Talk quietly. 4. The teacher gives everyone a
  timetable. Teacher: Here are today's timetables.
  5. At 1 o'clock, everyone has lunch in the museum
  café. But where is Rami? The teacher calls loudly,
  'Rami, Rami'. The children shout loudly, 'Rami,
  Rami!' Suzan is crying. Teacher: Rami, Rami! Where
  are you? Children: Rami! Rami! 6. At 2 o'clock,
  Rami comes back. He has a big smile on his face and
  is carrying the head of a big gold lion. Rami:
  Look! I made a lion! Teacher: Amazing! Girl: Rami,
  how did you make it? 7. Then everyone has a big
  surprise! [POP! balloon bursting] Boy: Ha, ha!
  Teacher: It was a balloon! Suzan: Rami bought a
  balloon! 8. After lunch, the children go to the
  museum shop to buy presents. They look at all the
  things on the shelves and think about how much
  money they have. Girl: I'm going to buy sweets.
  Suzan: I'm going to buy cookies and crayons. Rami:
  I'm not going to buy any more balloons!"
- **Ice-creams for everyone! (Track 148)** — the
  check-your-cloze telling; its closing lines as
  scripted: "Narrator: Lina had a mango ice-cream.
  Yousif wanted a chocolate one. Lina: This is great!
  … Narrator: Then it was time to go home. Old lady:
  You must come and see me. Yousif: We're going to
  come every week and have ice-cream!" (The Pupils
  Book text of the story stands as filed.)
- Official answers kept where they add facts:
  Adel's Check-my-progress email — 1 on, 2 do, 3
  good, 4 need, 5 and, 6 is, 7 big; "Read about you"
  — 1 I went shopping. 3 I bought a notebook and some
  pens. 4 I saw a film (about the fastest car in the
  world). 5 I ate ice-cream and drank lemonade.
- Method notes: the Book Map at the front of the PB
  "is not intended for pupils to use but it will be
  helpful for parents"; the AB "can be used as
  extended learning for pupils to do in their own
  time… to encourage independent learning"; stories
  get a weekly half-lesson "story time" and a
  classroom book corner; Pelmanism (present/past card
  pairs) is the year's verb game.
- The Phonetic Chart (p.169) is the family's standard
  chart (vowels, diphthongs, consonants with example
  words), unchanged in kind from the earlier years.

# Libya — English Grade 6: Pupils Book, Activity Book

Downloaded from the grade-06 library record's own
URLs (liasinstitute.com) into the courses work
folder. The record holds **two** books only — no
Teacher Book is listed for Grade 6.

- Pupils Book: 60 pp, 12,051,829 B — **READ COMPLETE
  2026-10-25** (partial text layer; read in full from
  rendered pages, book p. = PDF p.). A **different
  course** from the Garnet "21st Century" line of
  Grades 1–5: "Primary 6 Pupil's Book", State of
  Libya, Ministry of Education, CERC, by **Terry
  Phillips, from original work by Jenny Quintana and
  Bob Marsden**, school year 2019–2020 (1440–1441
  AH). It is not a re-cover of any earlier book —
  checked against Grade 5 first, per the Grade 4
  lesson: different author, different cast, different
  art, 60 pages against 141.
- Honest level note, as printed: this book works far
  below the Garnet Grade 5 book. It re-teaches the
  alphabet and sounds group by group, clothes, town
  places, there is/are, have got, likes, clock times
  and numbers to 20 — a consolidation year in a
  simpler series, with its own cast: **Ali** (the
  central boy), **Nisrin** and her brother **Tarek**
  (11) and sister **Amna** (8), **Ellen** (10, from
  England) and her sister **Tamara** (12), Nabil,
  Fatima, Hasan, Ahmed, Hala, Zainab, Khalid (from
  Jordan), Basim (from Pakistan), Omar, Jack, Hana —
  and an eagle mascot who presents the writing pages.
  Six lessons per unit; every unit's Lesson 6 is a
  writing lesson built on the series' signature
  exercise, **"Read and find the differences"** —
  the same text printed twice, once in full forms
  with capitals and punctuation, once shortened or
  stripped, and the child finds what changed.

### Grade 6 Pupils Book — unit by unit

**Front pages.** Alphabet and Numbers (p.3): all 26
letter pairs in colour + digits 1 2 3 4 5 6 7 8 9 0.
Book map (pp.4–5): the eight units with their
lexical set, statements, questions, language focus,
writing letters and sounds, as filed per unit below.

**Unit 1 Clothes (pp.6–11).** L1: ten clothes words
over pictures (a skirt, a dress, a jumper, socks, a
shirt, shoes, a blouse, sandals, a jacket,
trousers). L2: Nabil and Fatima described, with
**colour mixing written as sums** — "black + white
= grey" (his shirt), "red + white = pink" (her
jacket). L3: His/Her turned into names — "His shirt
is grey. → Nabil's shirt · Her jacket is pink. →
Fatima's jacket" — and Whose asked and answered
("Whose shirt is grey? — Nabil's. · Whose trousers
are blue? — Fatima's."). L4: the sounds b p j k
coloured inside words (blouse, bed, rubber, pink,
apple, grape, jacket, jumper, juice, kitchen, skirt,
black) + introducing other children ("This is
Khalid. He's from Jordan. And this is Basim. He's
from Pakistan."). L5: two reading texts ("Nabil's
shirt is grey. His trousers are red. His socks are
white. His shoes are black." · "Zainab's blouse is
white. Her skirt is blue. Her socks are grey. Her
shoes are black.") + Listen: Who's this? over six
children. L6: trace b p k j and B P K J with numbered
strokes. Writing focus: b B, p P, k K, j J · sounds
/b/ /p/ /k/ /dʒ/.

**Unit 2 Places in Town (pp.12–17).** L1: ten
places (a school, a mosque, a flat, a house, a
beach, a post office, a mall, a supermarket, a cafe,
a playground). L2: a hand-drawn street map (Hama
Street, Flag Street, Beach Road, the sea) —
"Ahmed's house is in Flag Street. Nabil's flat is
in Beach Road. Where's Jack's house? Where's
Ellen's flat?" L3: a coloured map (the street here
named Hani Street) — "The mosque is **in** Hani
Street. The mosque is **opposite** the school. The
mosque is **next to** the cafe. The mosque is
**near** Hama Street." L4: f and s in words + "My
house is in Flag Street." L5: a dialog in full —
"Ahmed: Where's your house, Ali? Ali: It's in Flag
Street. Ahmed: Really? My house is in Flag Street,
too. Ali: Where? Is it near the school? Ahmed: No,
it isn't. It's next to the mosque. Ali: Oh. Is it
opposite the playground? Ahmed: That's right." — a
numbered-map listening ("Where is Fatima's house?
What about Hasan's?") and the Look! box "isn't = is
not". L6: trace f s and F S (street signs: Flag
Street, Farhat Street, Faluja Street).

**Unit 3 Geographic Locations (pp.18–23).** L1: a
landscape picture labelled 1–9 (mountain, valley,
desert, village, river, lake, town, city, sea).
L2: a **symbol map** with a compass rose (North,
East, South, West) — the child writes what each
symbol means (triangle mountain, wavy line river,
diamond town, square city, blob lake, circle
village, band beach, dashed line road). L3: Libya's
own map — "There is a big desert in the south.
There are big cities in the north." · "Is there a
big desert in the south? — Yes, there is. **It is
the Sahara.** · Are there big cities in the south?
— No, there aren't." + Look! "aren't = are not".
L4: v w y in words + real places named: "This is
the **Beka Valley** in Lebanon. This is the **White
Sea** in Russia. This is the **Yellow River** in
China." L5: a **picture-rebus listening text** —
sentences built from words, pictures and arrows to
complete by ear ("My name's ___. I'm [Libya]. I'm
from [Sirt]. It's a ● in the ↑ of the country. My
[house] is in the ↓ of the ●…") — ● = city, ↑ =
north, ↓ = south. L6: trace v w y and V W Y.

**Unit 4 Vocabulary Reinforcement Through
Categorization (pp.24–29)** — the book's own title
for a sorting unit. L1–2: "Study these categories"
— picture-only boards, no words printed: Rooms,
Colours, Family, Animals, Fruits and drinks,
Adjectives (shown as opposite pairs), Clothes,
Town, Places — the earlier vocabulary re-sorted by
kind. L3: a dialog over a bedroom picture ("ALI'S
ROOM" on the door): "Where's my jacket? — It's on
the chair in your room. — Thanks. And where are my
shoes? — They're in your room, too. They're near
the bed. — Thanks." L4: **the whole sound system
on one page** — the five vowel letters with two
words each (a flag, black · e bed, desk · i fish,
kitchen · o hot, orange · u mum, uncle) and the 21
consonant letters with two words each (b bed, book
· p pen, pencil · m mum, monkey · f fish, office ·
v village, valley · t toilet, tiger · d desk, dad ·
n nice, nasty · c cat, socks · k kitchen, monkey ·
j jumper, jacket · s sea, dress · h horse, hot · l
lemon, lake · r red, grape · w west, water · y
yellow, you). L5: favourites — "What's your
favourite colour, Nisrin? — Blue is my favourite
colour. — It's red. Blue is nice, too. — Really?" ·
"This is my favourite drink. — Really? What is it?
— Banana milk shake. — My favourite drink is
lemonade. — Lemonade is nice, too." L6: "Study
the alphabet" — 23 letter pairs on coloured
splashes (q, x and z are not on this page; all 26
stand on p.3).

**Revision 1 (pp.30–31).** Listen: Who is it? over
the six children in new clothes + Describe and
guess; a new street map to talk over (Flag Street,
New Street, Faluja Street); "Ellen: What's your
favourite animal? Nisrin: The tiger. What about
you?"; ask and answer over animal pictures.

**Unit 5 Personal Possessions (pp.32–37).** L1: ten
possessions (a camera, a television (TV), a
computer, a radio, a bicycle, a watch, a CD player,
a motorbike, a fax machine, a video). L2: Ali on
his bicycle, his things pictured beside him (video
tape, CD, floppy disk, film) — "He's got a watch.
He hasn't got a motorbike. Has he got a camera?
What about a computer?" + Look! "he's = he has ·
hasn't = has not". L3: the pattern grid in pictures
— He's got / He hasn't got / Has he got? turned
into I've got / I haven't got / Have you got? —
Look! "I've = I have · I haven't = I have not".
L4: **letter names grouped by their sound** — the
letters B C D E G P T V drawn as characters (their
names all end in the same /iː/ sound) in a joke
frame ("What's your name? — E.") and BED spelled
out of its own letters ("What's that? — It's my
bed."). L5: the Ellen–Nisrin interview in full —
"Ellen: How old are you, Nisrin? Nisrin: I'm 9.
Ellen: Have you got brothers and sisters? Nisrin:
Yes, I have. Ellen: How many? Nisrin: I've got 1
brother and 1 sister. Ellen: What are their names?
Nisrin: My brother's name is Tarek and my sister's
name is Amna. Ellen: How old are they? Nisrin: My
brother is 11 and my sister is 8. Nisrin: What
about you, Ellen? Ellen: I'm 10. I've got 1 sister.
Her name is Tamara. Nisrin: How old is she? Ellen:
She's 12. I haven't got a brother." — the book's
numbers work is 11 and 12, met here inside the
family answers. L6: find the differences — one
note in full forms ("I am 11. I have got 1 brother.
He is tall. I have got 2 sisters. They are short.")
against the same note contracted ("I'm 11. I've
got 1 brother. He's tall. I've got 2 sisters.
They're short.") + Look! table (I'm = I am · He's =
He is · She's = She is · They're = They are · I've
= I have) + trace x (fax).

**Unit 6 Time / Daily Life (pp.38–43).** L1:
"I get up at quarter past 7. I start school at
quarter to 9. I finish school at half past 3. I go
to bed at 9 o'clock." + seven clocks read in words
(three o'clock · quarter past five · quarter to one
· half past six · quarter to three · half past nine
· quarter past two). L2: **the clock face drawn as
a diagram** — the right half is "past" (with "just
after" marked just past the hour and "nearly" just
before the half), the left half is "to"; o'clock at
the top, quarter past at the right, half past at
the bottom, quarter to at the left + "What's the
time? — It's three o'clock." L3: the routine grid —
I start / I don't start / Do you start …? over
start, finish and go to bed. L4: the letter names
A H J K as a class register ("A? — Here, sir.") +
spelling a name to a teacher: "What's your name?
— It's Jack, sir. J-A-C-K." L5: Nisrin and Ellen
compare school times in full — "Nisrin: What time
do you start school in England, Ellen? Ellen: I
start at half past 8. Nisrin: That's late. Ellen:
When do you start school, then? Nisrin: At quarter
past 7. Ellen: That's early! When do you finish?
Nisrin: At 1 o'clock. Ellen: That's great. We don't
finish at 1 o'clock. Nisrin: Do you finish at 2
o'clock? Ellen: No! Nisrin: What time do you
finish? Ellen: At quarter to 4." — early and late
learned from the comparison itself. L6: find the
differences (do not/don't · is not/isn't · have
got/I've got · have not/haven't) + Look! table +
trace q and qu (quarter).

**Unit 7 Sports and Activities (pp.44–49).** L1:
ten activities (table tennis, basketball, football,
swimming, shopping, fishing, reading, cycling,
listening to the radio, watching TV). L2: like-maps
— "Nisrin likes table tennis but she doesn't like
football. Ali likes football but he doesn't like
table tennis." + "Does Nisrin like basketball? What
about Ali?" L3: the likes grid (She likes … / She
doesn't like … / Does she like …? · He likes …).
L4: **the letter-name dialog** — the letters I and
U talk, with Y, Q and W as their brothers: "I:
Hello. U: Hello. I: Who are you? U: I'm U. I: Are
you? U: Yes. Who are you? I: I'm I. U: Really?
Who's that? I: He's my brother. His name is Y. U:
They're my brothers, Q and W." — the joke is that
the letter names sound like words (I, you, are,
why). L5: a clothes-shop dialog in full — "Omar:
Do you like this red shirt? Ahmed: No, not really.
I don't like red. Omar: What about this one?
Ahmed: No, I don't like blue, either. Omar: I like
this one. Yellow is my favourite colour. Ahmed:
Yes, I like it, too. Omar: Well? What do you think?
Ahmed: It's too big, isn't it?" — too, either and
too big, with a tag question at the end. L6: find
the differences for **capital letters and full
stops** — Ali's self-introduction printed correctly
("My name is Ali. I'm African. I'm from Libya. My
town is Sirt. I've got a brother and I've got a
sister. My brother's name is Mousa. My sister's
name is Huda.") against the same text in all small
letters with no full stops; Look! "my name = My
name … · it's = It's … · i'm = I'm … · ali = Ali ·
libya = Libya · sirt = Sirt".

**Unit 8 Games (pp.50–55).** L1: **Snakes and
Ladders** — a 20-square board, every square carrying
its number in figures and in words (one … twenty),
and the rules as imperatives: "1. Spin the spinner.
2. Move your counter. 3. Go up a ladder. 4. Go down
a snake. 5. If you get a six, spin again." L2: the
game played in speech, in full — "Jack: Where are
you? Ali: I'm on three. Jack: OK. Spin the spinner.
Ali: I've got a six! Jack: Four, five, six, seven,
eight, nine. You're on a ladder. Go up to eleven.
Ali: That's good. Your turn. Jack: No, it isn't.
It's your turn again. Ali: Oh, yes. Now I've got a
four. Jack: Twelve, thirteen, fourteen, fifteen …
You're on a snake! Ali: Oh, no! Jack: Go down to
seven!" L3: **How do you make a spinner?** — seven
imperative steps, a picture for each: "1. Draw a
line. 2. Draw an X on the line. 3. Join the points.
4. Write the numbers one to six. 5. Colour the
spinner. 6. Cut out the spinner. 7. Push a pencil
through the middle." L4: the letter characters at
school again — the F L M N S X family (their names
all begin with the same /e/ sound) out front:
"Are you E? — No, I'm F." L5: the game taught by
playing it — "Nisrin: How do you play snakes and
ladders? Ellen: First, spin the spinner. Nisrin:
OK. Ellen: What have you got? Nisrin: Four. Ellen:
Right. Move your counter four squares. Nisrin: One,
two, three, four. Nisrin: There's a ladder on this
square. Ellen: OK. Go up the ladder. Nisrin: Where
to? Ellen: To six. Now it's my turn." L6: find the
differences for **punctuation** — the game's rules
printed correctly against the same rules in small
letters with no marks; Look! "a = small letter · A
= capital letter · . = full stop · , = comma · ? =
question mark".

**Revision 2 (pp.56–57).** L1: "Look and remember"
— one big cutaway picture of a sports centre
(football, basketball, table tennis, an office, a
changing room, fishing and cycling outside) to study
and then talk about from memory. L2: Nisrin's
self-introduction as a **picture rebus** to read,
then "Write. I'm …" — your own; a Nisrin–Hana
dialog ("Do you like this dress? — No, not really.
I don't like green. · Do you like these shoes? —
Yes, I do. They're nice."); ask and answer over six
clothes pictures marked ✓ or ✗.

**Picture Dictionary (pp.58–60).** Clothes (10) ·
Town (10) · Places (city, desert, mountain, river,
town, valley, village) · "Have you got a …?" (the
ten possessions) · Daily routine (get up, start
school, finish school, go to bed) · "Do you like
…?" (the ten activities — printed here as
"listening to **music**" where Unit 7 says
"listening to the **radio**") · Games (counter, go
down, go up, ladder, move, spin, spinner).

→ Filed/recorded from this book: colour mixing as
sums (black + white = grey · red + white = pink);
map symbols with a compass; the Sahara named in a
there-is answer; the Beka Valley, the White Sea and
the Yellow River; picture-rebus reading; short forms
taught by find-the-differences (he's = he has ·
I've = I have · isn't/aren't/don't/haven't) and
capitals and punctuation taught the same way, with
the marks named (small letter, capital letter, full
stop, comma, question mark); letter names grouped
by sound and played as characters (B C D E G P T V ·
A H J K · I Y U W Q · F L M N S X); the clock-face
past/to diagram with "just after" and "nearly";
England-vs-Libya school times (half past 8 start,
quarter to 4 finish · quarter past 7 start, 1
o'clock finish); Snakes and Ladders with its rules
and its playing talk; and how to make a spinner in
seven steps. Acceptance counts unchanged — same
country, an older simpler series spiralling the same
basics.

**Libya Grade 6 Pupils Book — READ COMPLETE
2026-10-25.**

### Grade 6 Activity Book

96 pp, 5,364,848 B, image-only — **READ COMPLETE
2026-10-25** (read in full from rendered pages;
book p. = PDF p.). The black-and-white writing twin:
every Pupils unit gets Lessons 1–6 in the Pupils
order **plus a Lesson 7 the Pupils Book does not
have** (letter-recognition drills in U1–U3, a numbers
lesson in U5, ordering lessons in U6–U8), and it
cross-references the Pupils Book by page ("Look at
Pupil's Book page 26 and complete", "Look at Pupil's
Book page 56. What's missing?"). Imprint page:
"English for Libya, Primary 6 Activity Book —
Materials development by **Garnet Publishing Ltd**,
8 Southern Court, South Street, Reading RG1 4QS, UK
— This edition **first published 2007, Reprinted
2018**", code **EFL AB5 18** — so the series is an
older Garnet course reprinted for 2019–2020, which
is why its level sits below the 2019 "21st Century"
Grades 1–5 line.

Filed by unit: **U1** — match words to pictures;
cover and say; colour and ask ("What colour is the
jacket? — It's blue." / "Is the jacket blue? — Yes,
it is. / No, it isn't. It's red."); "What's missing?"
chunk completion; a Venn sort (Nabil / both /
Zainab — the heading says "Nabil or Fatima — or
either?" but the diagram names Zainab, as printed);
colour-mix sentences to complete with a bank (Red
and yellow is ___. … Red and yellow and blue is
___. — answers run orange, grey, green, pink,
brown); sentence-building clouds (owner + clothes +
is/are + colour); circle the odd one out (8 rows);
sound ticks /b/ vs /p/ and /j/ vs /k/ (the book
prints the j sound as /j/); "Write c, k or ck"
(_itchen, _amel, bla__, pin_, coo_er, milk sha_e,
sna_e, ja__et); Read and colour — the Pupils
description texts extended to all six children;
letter copy rows and fill-ins (a__le, ru__er, Na_il,
_akistan, Zaina_, gra_e, _umper, s_irt, mon_ey,
_halid, _uice, _itchen, sin_ = sink, _ordan, _ac_et);
L7 recognition drills ("Yes or no — b?" circle the
true letter among near-miss shapes; letter strings
to sort; "How many?").
**U2** — join the word halves (play+ground …
ca+fe, ma+ll); "Where is Ali?" — nine "He isn't in
the ___" sentences whose numbered letters spell
where he IS; guess-your-partner's-place frames;
read and number three house descriptions (Omar's,
Sami's, Jamal's — new names); /s/ vs /f/ ticks and
an is/this listening tick; write s, ss or c; the
Pupils dialog as a put-in-order task (one line
changed here: "It's next to the **school**" where
the Pupils Book says mosque — as printed) +
complete-the-conversation + match Q&A; f F s S rows
(the "Write s." row prints capital-S shapes, as
printed); an alphabet-gap "Revision" page; L7 f/s
drills + word copying.
**U3** — a wordsearch (mountain found diagonally as
the example); sentence clouds (The [village/town/
city] is [in/on/near] the [sea/mountains/river/
lake]); anagrams of the place words; **a treasure-
map listening** — follow spoken directions ("north
3, east 4, north 3, west 1…") across a grid; a

shortshoescafe); vowel completion against a vowel

are mountains in the east."), draw a map, pair

**one letter, two sounds** sorts ("y = your or
city?", "w = west or yellow?"); match Q&A; label
the compass rose's in-between points; listen and
number four Libyan cities on the map (**Tubruq,
Tripoli, Ghat, Sirt**); and a cloze text with a
**real photograph of Ghat**: "Ghat is ___ small
town. It is ___ the southwest of Libya. It is ___
the Tassili mountains. It is ___ the Abu Ata
mountains. It is near ___ Sahara Desert, too."
(bank: in, near, a, the); v V w W y Y rows; capital
alphabet revision; L7 v/w/y drills + copying.
**U4** — sort a word bank into nine labelled
category rows, crossing words out as used; a
clothes crossword whose shaded column spells a
hidden word; build-a-question frame from category
words; unscramble and write questions; the two-pass
room completion from Pupil's Book p.26 (nouns first,
then is/a/the/near/on/There/too); **odd one out by
